\title{Renminbi Exchange-Rate Expectations and China's Foreign Exchange
Reserves: A Replication of Du, Ge and Qiao (2014)\thanks{Contact:
Jiancheng Du, jiancheng.du@okstate.edu. Code, public data and the
decision index are in the replication package,
https://github.com/du-jc/fer-replication.}}
\author{Jiancheng Du}

\maketitle


\section*{Abstract}\label{abstract}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Abstract}

Du, Ge and Qiao (2014) estimate the relation between expectations of the
renminbi--dollar exchange rate and the growth of China's foreign
exchange reserves, 2000-2011, with a model-based expectation (Model I)
and a market-based expectation using the 12-month non-deliverable
forward rate (NDF; Model II), in four exchange-rate regimes. From the
data of the original document we reproduce 18 of 20 Model I and 23 of 24
Model II estimates by sign and significance, most because both estimates
are insignificant. We correct standard errors labelled as
\emph{t}-statistics, an inflation growth factor used as a deflator,
year-to-date GDP, coefficients read as percentages, standard errors that
ignore the generated regressor, and Chow statistics that cannot be
reproduced. The article's one significant finding, the NDF coefficient
for 2008-2011, survives every per-period correction, but it is negative:
reserves grew faster when appreciation was priced. Model I shows no
robust expectation effect.

\textbf{Keywords:} replication, exchange-rate expectations, foreign
exchange reserves, China, generated regressors, non-deliverable
forwards.

\textbf{JEL classification:} F31, F32, E58, C22, C87.

\textbf{Conflict of interest:} the author of this note is one of the
authors of the study replicated here. The research was not funded by any
party with an interest in its outcome.

\textbf{Use of AI tools and purpose of this report:} this replication
was carried out mainly to conduct a full replication and reproducibility
procedure using AI tools. The author used Claude Code (Anthropic) to
draft planning documents, write the data-collection and estimation code,
propose options for analytic decisions, and draft much of the text of
this note. \textbf{This report is therefore not intended for submission
or publication.} The AI tool supplied no data value, estimate or result:
every number in this note is produced by code that reads the original
sources and was run by the author. Every analytic decision was taken by
the author and recorded in the dated decision log before the estimates
it governs (Appendix A), except where this note declares a later change.
The author reviewed all code and text and is responsible for them.
Details are in \texttt{docs/ai\_use.md} of the replication package.

\section{Introduction}\label{sec-intro}

Du, Ge and Qiao (2014) estimate how expectations about the renminbi
(RMB)--US dollar exchange rate affected the accumulation of China's
foreign exchange reserves (FER) between 2000 and 2011. They split the
sample into four exchange-rate regimes and measure expectations in two
ways: a forecast generated from a three-stage regression model (Model I)
and the 12-month non-deliverable forward rate (NDF, Model II). The
abstract states that both methods ``indicated that the change of
expectation of RMB exchange rate positively correlated to FER''
(p.~327). The conclusions state that the expectation ``had a significant
impact on the FER recently'', as shown in Period 4 (June 2008 to
December 2011), and that ``before June 2006, the change of RMB exchange
rate expectation did not affect the FER significantly because of
exchange rate management'' (p.~341). Of the eight published expectation
coefficients, those marked as significant are: Model II, Period 4
(article Table 4, p.~339).

How much of China's reserve accumulation was driven by expected
appreciation was part of a wider debate (Prasad and Wei 2007). Under a
managed exchange rate, an expected appreciation invites capital inflows,
the central bank buys the foreign currency to slow the appreciation, and
reserves grow; the purchases must then be sterilised at a cost (Obstfeld
et al. 2010). Reserves were also explained as precautionary insurance or
as a by-product of an export-led strategy (Aizenman and Lee 2007). An
expectation effect, if it exists, therefore bears on whether reserve
accumulation was a policy choice or a response to market pressure.

This note is a replication in the sense of the \emph{Journal of Comments
and Replications in Economics}: it assesses the reliability of the
article's claims. We do two things. First, we \textbf{reproduce} the
published estimates from the data of the original document on which the
article is based, which contains the full dataset and every test and
estimation table (Section~\ref{sec-reproduction}). Second, we
\textbf{correct} twelve problems in the original analysis, one at a
time, and show how each correction changes the estimated effect of
expectations (Sections \ref{sec-corrections} and \ref{sec-effect}).

\textbf{Disclosure.} The author of this note is one of the authors of
the original article. To prevent the replication from being steered
towards a preferred result, every rule that selects data, specifications
or classifications was written down, dated, \emph{before} the estimates
it governs were computed, and the code and results of each step were
committed to git. These rules include which balance-of-payments series
is used, which unit-root specification, which Model I specification
counts as the reproduction, and when an estimate counts as reproduced.
One of these rules was changed after estimation: the differencing
convention. The rule fixed before estimation measured the first change
of each period from the last month of the previous period. A condition
for switching to differencing within each period was stated in advance
(W4-11), the check it named was met (W4-25), and the switch was made and
is reported, with results under the original rule as a sensitivity check
(W4-27, W4-31). One correction outside the replication plan, a constant
in stage 2 of Model I, was added after the first corrected estimates had
been seen, and is reported as a separate step (W4-20). Two diagnoses in
this note were also found after the results were seen and are marked as
such (W4-26).

Each rule is recorded, with the options considered and the evidence, in
a numbered decision log, which Appendix A lists. The log is kept off the
public repository and is available on request. All code and all public
data are available at https://github.com/du-jc/fer-replication; the only
inputs that cannot be redistributed are the original document and the
Wind NDF series (Section~\ref{sec-availability}).

Section~\ref{sec-original} describes the original study,
Section~\ref{sec-data} the data, Section~\ref{sec-reproduction} the
reproduction, Section~\ref{sec-corrections} the corrections, and
Section~\ref{sec-effect} their effect on the conclusions.
Section~\ref{sec-availability} explains how to reproduce every result,
and Section~\ref{sec-conclusion} concludes.

\section{The original study}\label{sec-original}

\subsection{Models}\label{models}

The article estimates two models of the monthly change in reserves,
\(\Delta FER_t\), separately in four periods. \textbf{Model I} builds an
expected change of the exchange rate in three stages. Stage 1 regresses
the change of the exchange rate (RMB per USD) on fundamentals:

\begin{equation}\protect\phantomsection\label{eq-stage1}{
\Delta ER_t = b_0 + b_1\,\Delta BOP_t + b_2\,\Delta i_t + b_3\,\Delta \pi_t + b_4\,\Delta GDP_t + x_t .
}\end{equation}

Stage 2 forecasts the change of the exchange rate from its own past,
without a constant. The original document is ambiguous about which
series it projects: its equation (4-2) regresses the observed change on
its own lags, while its tables label the stage-3 regressor with a double
hat, which indicates the stage-1 fitted values. We estimate both. The
primary form follows the double hat, the stage-1 fitted values, which
the original authors intended.

\begin{equation}\protect\phantomsection\label{eq-stage2}{
\widehat{\Delta ER}_t = \sum_{s=1}^{j} a_s\,\widehat{\Delta ER}_{t-s} + e_t  \qquad \text{(primary)},
}\end{equation}

\begin{equation}\protect\phantomsection\label{eq-stage2b}{
\Delta ER_t = \sum_{s=1}^{j} a_s\, \Delta ER_{t-s} + e_t  \qquad \text{(alternative, as printed in equation (4-2))},
}\end{equation}

with one to three lags. The headline specification
(Section~\ref{sec-reproduction}) uses the alternative form with 1
lag(s). In the alternative form the stage-3 regressor is a weighted sum
of the past exchange-rate changes and does not depend on stage 1.

Stage 3 uses the fitted value \(\widehat{\widehat{\Delta ER}}_t\) as the
expectation:

\begin{equation}\protect\phantomsection\label{eq-stage3}{
\Delta FER_t = \tau_0 + \tau_1\,\widehat{\widehat{\Delta ER}}_t + \tau_2\,\Delta NX_t + \tau_3\,\Delta FDB_t + \tau_4\,\Delta FDI_t + y_t .
}\end{equation}

The coefficients of stages 1 and 2 are estimated from all months of a
period, so the fitted value for month \emph{t} uses information from
months after \emph{t}. It is an in-sample fitted value, not an
expectation that could have been formed at the time, although the
article calls it a rational expectation (p.~327; p.~332). This holds for
both forms of the stage-2 regressor and both differencing conventions
(W4-17). This is a limitation of Model I that we do not correct in this
note; Section~\ref{sec-conclusion} proposes how to address it in future
work.

\textbf{Model II} replaces the generated expectation by the observed
12-month NDF:

\begin{equation}\protect\phantomsection\label{eq-model2}{
\Delta FER_t = g_0 + g_1\,\Delta NDF_t + g_2\,\Delta GDP_t + g_3\,\Delta NX_t + g_4\,\Delta FDB_t + g_5\,\Delta FDI_t + w_t .
}\end{equation}

The variables are defined in Table~\ref{tbl-variables}. In the original
document, the regressions of Periods 1 and 3 have AR(1) errors
(ARIMA(1,1,0)) and those of Periods 2 and 4 are estimated by OLS. The
periods follow the exchange-rate regime (Table~\ref{tbl-periods}).

The article's conceptual model (its Figure 1, p.~332) lets reserves
affect the exchange rate and the expected exchange rate affect reserves.
The estimated equations (p.~334) do not identify this loop: stage 1
omits reserves (equation \ref{eq-stage1}), and each equation is
estimated by single-equation methods. None of the estimated coefficients
can therefore be read as a causal effect of expectations on reserves;
they measure associations (W4-21). The original document itself names
simultaneous equations as the approach for future research (Section
4.5), which is also a future research direction we propose in this
report.

\subsection{Reported findings}\label{reported-findings}

The article reads its Table 4 (p.~339) in Section 4.3 (p.~340-341). It
reports that both expectation measures have positive coefficients in
Periods 1 and 2, and negative ones in Periods 3 and 4, and attributes
the change to the regime: before June 2006 the RMB was ``managed or
simply peg on the dollar'', after it ``a basket of currency policy was
adopted'' (p.~341). Of the expectation coefficients, it reports only the
NDF coefficient of Model II in Period 4 as significant, at the 5\% level
(p.~340-341), and calls it ``positive'' in one sentence and ``negative''
in another (Section~\ref{sec-corrections}, sign convention). Other
coefficients are read as percentage effects (p.~340). From Chow tests,
the article concludes that the determinants of reserves changed
structurally between the periods (conclusion 2, p.~342), and it
recommends ``decreasing the expectation of RMB exchange rate and the
amount of foreign debt balance'' to keep reserves at a moderate scale
(conclusion 3, p.~342).

\section{Data}\label{sec-data}

\subsection{The original data}\label{the-original-data}

All data and all reproduction targets are read by code from the original
document; nothing is transcribed by hand. Appendix I of the original
document supplies nine monthly variables for the 144 months from January
2000 to December 2011 (Table~\ref{tbl-variables}). The extracted data
were checked against the original study's descriptive statistics (Tables
3.2 to 3.9): of the 304 cells of those tables, 267 agree to the printed
decimals on the raw data (decision W2-13; tolerance: half a unit of the
last printed digit, W2-07). The cells that do not agree to the printed
decimals are of three kinds (W2-13). The Period 1 correlations involving
the exchange rate are printed in the original document although the raw
exchange rate is constant in Period 1, so these correlations are
undefined; they are discrepancies of the original document. The Period 2
correlations involving the exchange rate, and a few means, standard
deviations and correlations in other periods, differ from ours by small
amounts. With the tolerance of 0.05, set after the comparison was seen
(W2-07), only the Period 1 correlations remain unmatched. Our
interpretation suggests that the small gaps arise because the original
computed its statistics from data with more decimals than the appendix
prints. This does not explain the Period 1 correlations, which are
undefined on the raw data.

\subsection{Inputs not in the original
appendix}\label{inputs-not-in-the-original-appendix}

Two regressors are not in the appendix and were collected anew, and
price data were added for the corrections (Table~\ref{tbl-sources};
State Administration of Foreign Exchange (2026); Wind Information Co.
(2026); International Monetary Fund (2026); Organisation for Economic
Co-operation and Development (2026); National Bureau of Statistics of
China (2026)).

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Balance of payments.} The quarterly current account (SAFE,
  BPM6 presentation) is converted to months. Of three candidate
  conversions (quarterly value held, divided by three, year-to-date),
  the one used, bop\_ca\_ytd, was chosen by a rule fixed before any
  Model I estimate. The rule combines the distance to the original
  unit-root statistic and to the four stage-1 Durbin--Watson statistics
  (W2-01, W2-10, W2-12).\footnote{The original document predates SAFE's
    BPM6 presentation and probably used BPM5 data. For the current
    account, the series used here, the BPM5 and BPM6 are identical over
    the sample period (W2-12), so the accounting standard does not
    explain a mismatch.}
\item
  \textbf{NDF.} The 12-month USD/CNY NDF is the only Wind tenor that
  covers 2000--2011. Month-end and month-average versions of the daily
  series were compared with the original unit-root statistic before
  Model II was estimated; the version used is ndf\_mend (W2-06, W2-10).
\item
  \textbf{Consumer prices.} For the deflator correction
  (Section~\ref{sec-deflator}), a fixed-base consumer price index for
  China was taken from the IMF data portal and cross-checked against
  FRED (OECD data). The NBS published year-on-year index (same month of
  the previous year = 100) was used to identify the original study's
  inflation series (W4-05, W4-14).
\end{itemize}

\section{Reproduction}\label{sec-reproduction}

\subsection{Procedure}\label{procedure}

The reproduction follows rules fixed before estimation. Because the
original document does not fully document Model I, all twelve
combinations of its undocumented choices were estimated. These choices
are whether stage 2 projects the stage-1 fitted values or the observed
exchange rate, the number of lags (one to three), and whether the
variables are used as published or divided by \(\pi/100\), as the
article states. The combination that reproduces most published cells was
then named the headline (W3-02). Each estimate is classified on its sign
and star level only (W1-07), with the meanings of *, ** and *** taken
from the notes of the original table 4 (p.~339) and significance meaning
\emph{p} \textless{} 0.10; the dagger, which those notes do not define
and which appears once in the original table, is read as \emph{p}
\textless{} 0.10 (W4-16). An estimate is \textbf{reproduced} if it has
the same sign and the same star level as the published one, or if both
are insignificant; \textbf{partially reproduced} if both are significant
with the same sign but a different star level; and \textbf{not
reproduced} if significance flips, or if the signs differ and either
estimate is significant. Magnitude, measured as the gap in published
standard errors, is reported but does not affect the class. First
differences, and the lags of stage 2, are taken within each period, as
if each period were a separate dataset, so that the first month of each
period is lost to differencing (the ``within'' convention). This is a
change made after estimation. The convention fixed beforehand took
differences on the whole 2000-2011 series, so that the first change of a
period was measured from the last month of the previous one (W3-01). The
switch followed a condition stated in advance, that the Durbin-Watson
bounds printed in the original document should imply within-period
samples (W4-11, W4-25, W4-27). Both conventions use only current and
past months. Results under full-sample differencing are reported as a
sensitivity check (W4-31).

\subsection{Unit-root tests}\label{unit-root-tests}

The article (Du et al. 2014) and the text of the original document (its
Section 4.1.1) describe the unit-root test as an augmented Dickey-Fuller
(ADF) test; the tables of the original document report DF-GLS (Elliott
et al. 1996). The original document does not state its lag rule. The
specification that best reproduces the original statistics of the eight
appendix variables was selected and then fixed (W2-02, W2-09).
Table~\ref{tbl-unitroot} shows the original statistics, our DF-GLS
statistics and the ADF statistics at the same lag. DF-GLS and ADF agree
on rejection at 5\% in 14 of 14 series. Our DF-GLS statistics agree with
the original's to the printed decimals for some series and differ
slightly for the others (Table~\ref{tbl-unitroot}). No stationarity
judgment at the 5\% level differs from the original's (W2-09). The
specification that achieves this closeness uses a fixed lag of one,
which is not a documented lag-selection rule; it was chosen because it
reproduces the original statistics, and it is reported as such.

\textsuperscript{a} Original DF-GLS: original Tables 4.1 (levels) and

in Week 2 (deflated/trend/common/fixed\_1) and the same number of
lags.\\
\textsuperscript{b} Critical values at 5\%: DF-GLS from urca (Elliott,
Rothenberg and Stock 1996 tables), ADF from urca (Dickey-Fuller tables).
The original's critical values (Stata dfgls, finite-sample) differ by
construction; compare the statistics.\\
\textsuperscript{c} Rejects: the unit-root null is rejected at 5\%.

\subsection{Estimates}\label{estimates}

The headline Model I specification uses the alternative stage-2
regressor with 1 lag(s) on deflated data. It reproduces 18 of the 20
Model I cells; 1 are partially reproduced and 1 not reproduced. The
ranking among the 12 combinations was decided by count (W3-07). For
Model II on deflated data, 23 of 24 cells are reproduced (W3-08).
Because two insignificant estimates count as reproduced whatever their
size, the counts rest largely on such cells (W3-14): of the reproduced
Model I cells, 14 are insignificant in both the original and our
estimates, and 13 lie within one published standard error of the
published value; for Model II, 18 and 21. Tables \ref{tbl-repro-a} and
\ref{tbl-repro-b} (Tables 4.9 and 4.10 in the original document; Table 4
in the article) set the published and the reproduced estimates side by
side, and Figure~\ref{fig-gap} shows each gap in units of the published
standard error.

\textsuperscript{a} Published: original Table 4.9 as printed (marks as
printed); the numbers in parentheses are those printed below each
coefficient, which the article labels t-statistics (see the section
`Standard errors labelled as t-statistics').\\
\textsuperscript{b} Reproduced: our estimates, Model I with the
alternative stage-2 regressor and 1 lag(s) on deflated data (rank 1
under the pre-specified rule W3-02; result W3-07); Model II on the same
data; differences on the within convention. Standard errors in
parentheses: OPG for ARIMA periods, OLS otherwise.\\
\textsuperscript{c} n.i. = not identified: the regressor has no
variation in that period (the exchange rate was pegged in Period 1).\\
\textsuperscript{d} Estimator: ARIMA = regression in first differences
with AR(1) errors (ARIMA(1,1,0) in levels), exact maximum likelihood;
OLS otherwise.\\
\textsuperscript{e} Our coefficients are rounded to the decimals printed
in the original.\\
\textsuperscript{f} Shading of the reproduced estimates (classification
W1-07, on sign and star level only; significant = p \textless{} 0.10):
green = reproduced (same sign and same star level, or both
insignificant); yellow = partially reproduced (same sign, both
significant, different star level); red = not reproduced (significance
flips, signs differ with either significant, or not identified).\\
\textsuperscript{g} Significance (legend of original Tables 4.9-4.10): †
p \textless{} 0.1, * p \textless{} 0.05, ** p \textless{} 0.01, *** p
\textless{} 0.001 (two-sided; normal for the ARIMA periods, t for
OLS).\\
\textsuperscript{h} Periods: 1 = 2000-01 to 2005-06; 2 = 2005-07 to
2006-05; 3 = 2006-06 to 2008-05; 4 = 2008-06 to 2011-12 (Replication
Plan s.3.3).

\renewcommand*{\arraystretch}{1}

\textsuperscript{a} Published: original Table 4.10 as printed (marks as
printed); the numbers in parentheses are those printed below each
coefficient, which the article labels t-statistics (see the section
`Standard errors labelled as t-statistics').\\
\textsuperscript{b} Reproduced: our estimates, Model I with the
alternative stage-2 regressor and 1 lag(s) on deflated data (rank 1
under the pre-specified rule W3-02; result W3-07); Model II on the same
data; differences on the within convention. Standard errors in
parentheses: OPG for ARIMA periods, OLS otherwise.\\
\textsuperscript{c} n.i. = not identified: the regressor has no
variation in that period (the exchange rate was pegged in Period 1).\\
\textsuperscript{d} Estimator: ARIMA = regression in first differences
with AR(1) errors (ARIMA(1,1,0) in levels), exact maximum likelihood;
OLS otherwise.\\
\textsuperscript{e} Our coefficients are rounded to the decimals printed
in the original.\\
\textsuperscript{f} Shading of the reproduced estimates (classification
W1-07, on sign and star level only; significant = p \textless{} 0.10):
green = reproduced (same sign and same star level, or both
insignificant); yellow = partially reproduced (same sign, both
significant, different star level); red = not reproduced (significance
flips, signs differ with either significant, or not identified).\\
\textsuperscript{g} Significance (legend of original Tables 4.9-4.10): †
p \textless{} 0.1, * p \textless{} 0.05, ** p \textless{} 0.01, *** p
\textless{} 0.001 (two-sided; normal for the ARIMA periods, t for
OLS).\\
\textsuperscript{h} Periods: 1 = 2000-01 to 2005-06; 2 = 2005-07 to
2006-05; 3 = 2006-06 to 2008-05; 4 = 2008-06 to 2011-12 (Replication
Plan s.3.3).

\begin{figure}[H]

\centering{

\includegraphics[width=1\linewidth,height=\textheight,keepaspectratio]{../outputs/figures/fig1_std_gap.png}

}

\caption{\label{fig-gap}Gap between reproduced and published Model I
estimates, in published standard errors (headline specification). Dashed
lines: one standard error. Cells beyond the clipped axis are marked with
triangles and labelled with their gap. Source: own estimates; original
Tables 4.9-4.10.}

\end{figure}%

The controls and the autoregressive error terms reproduce closely: of
the 18 reproduced Model I cells, 13 lie within one published standard
error of the published value. The expectation term does not. In each of
the 4 periods its published and its reproduced coefficient are both
insignificant, so the cell counts as reproduced under our rule (W1-07),
but the sign differs in 2 of them and the gaps are large
(Figure~\ref{fig-gap}). Our interpretation suggests that the expectation
term is the least reproducible part of Model I because it is a generated
regressor: its values depend on choices that the original document does
not report, namely the series that stage 2 projects, the lag order,
whether stage 2 has a constant, and how the period samples were built
(W3-02, W4-18, W4-19). Model II, whose expectation measure is observed
rather than generated, reproduces closely, including its one significant
expectation coefficient, in Period 4: -1,294.45* (SE 531.20) published
and -1,288.88* (SE 531.10) reproduced.

The one Model I cell that is not reproduced, net external debt in Period
4, lies at the significance threshold. The published coefficient and
standard error imply \emph{p} = 0.095 (normal approximation), just below
the 10\% level at which our rule counts an estimate as significant, yet
the published table leaves it unmarked: its legend defines marks only
from 5\% and a 10\% mark appears once in the original tables (W4-16).
Our estimate lies 0.22 published standard errors from the published
value. Our interpretation suggests that this cell reflects the marking
threshold, not a different estimate.

The constants of Periods 1 and 3 reproduce in sign and significance but
not in size: ours are between 100 and 122 times the published values, in
both models (Table~\ref{tbl-repro-a}, Table~\ref{tbl-repro-b}; W3-07).
We examined two explanations.

The first is a constant in stage 2, which the printed equations
(\ref{eq-stage2}, \ref{eq-stage2b}) omit but a default regression
command would include. Before re-estimating, we fixed the rule for
reading the result (W4-19). The explanation would count as likely if,
with a stage-2 constant, both constants came within one published
standard error of the published values and no other Model I cell changed
class; as partial if both moved closer; and as ruled out otherwise. For
the headline specification (alternative, lag 1, deflated), with a
stage-2 constant (row 1c of Table~\ref{tbl-steps-1}), the Period 1
constant lies 300.74 published standard errors from the published value
(283.08 without it) and the Period 3 constant 1618.55 (521.84); 0 other
Model I cells change class. By the rule, the outcome is (c): the missing
stage-2 constant is ruled out as the explanation of the constant gaps.
The outcome is the same under the convention fixed before estimation
(W4-22, W4-29).

The second explanation is a scaling of the data. The article states that
``all variables are divided by inflation which is noted as π'' (p.~334),
where π is an index close to 100, while our deflated data, like the data
printed in the article (Table 2, p.~335), divided by π/100. Dividing
every variable by π instead of π/100 multiplies all of them by 1/100.
Such a common rescaling leaves every slope, every autoregressive
coefficient and every \emph{t} statistic unchanged and divides the
constant and its standard error by 100, which is the pattern in Tables
\ref{tbl-repro-a} and \ref{tbl-repro-b}. Rescaled by 1/100, our
constants lie the following numbers of published standard errors from
the published ones: Model I, Period 1: 0.01; Model I, Period 3: 0.96;
Model II, Period 1: 0.00; Model II, Period 3: 0.00; 4 of the 4 are
within one standard error. This diagnosis was found after the results
were seen (W4-26). Our interpretation suggests that the original
regressions used the variables divided by π, as the article's text says,
and that this accounts for the size of the published constants; no slope
coefficient, and hence no conclusion of the article, is affected.

The sample sizes printed in the original document are matched in 4 of 28
comparisons (W3-11). Two further pieces of evidence bear on how the
original built its period samples. First, the original prints the same
number of observations for Model I as for Model II in Periods 1 and 3,
although the stage-2 lags remove months from the Model I sample under
either differencing convention as we implement it (W4-18). Second, the
Durbin-Watson bounds printed in original Table 4.3 identify the sample
size \emph{n} and the number of regressors \emph{k} of the stage-1
regressions, because the exact 5\% bounds differ for every pair
(\emph{n}, \emph{k}) (W4-25; Table~\ref{tbl-dwbounds}). In Period 1 they
imply the sample that both conventions give. In Period 2 they match the
within-period sample of the stage-1 regression exactly. In Period 3 they
match the within-period sample size, with 3 regressors instead of the 4
of stage 1. In Period 4 they imply \emph{n} = 40 and \emph{k} = 3, which
fit neither convention (43 full, 42 within). No period points to
full-sample differencing, and this is the evidence on which the primary
convention was changed (W4-27). Our interpretation suggests, as
hypotheses: in Period 4 the bounds may have been read from a printed
table at a nearby tabulated sample size rather than the actual one (one
possible reason, not verified); and the original differenced within each
period, at least for stage 1 and for Model II, while its Model I
expectation regressor was available for every month of a period.

\subsection{Autocorrelation tests}\label{autocorrelation-tests}

The original study tests residual autocorrelation with the
Durbin--Watson statistic and, where it is inconclusive, with the
Breusch--Godfrey test (original Tables 4.3 to 4.8). Over all tables,
data versions and conventions, 1 of 168 statistics agree with the
printed values to the printed decimals, and 124 of 168 test decisions
agree (W3-12). On deflated data under the primary convention, the
decisions agree in 38 of 42 cases. Our interpretation suggests that the
original tested regressions that differ from ours in their samples or
regressors. The bounds printed in original Table 4.3 imply the sample
size and the number of regressors of each stage-1 regression
(Table~\ref{tbl-dwbounds}), and in Periods 3 and 4 these differ from the
stage-1 regression as specified. A test decision is robust to such
differences when the statistic lies far from the critical values; the
statistic itself is not. We therefore compare the decisions and not the
statistics.

\subsection{Chow tests}\label{chow-tests}

The original study reports Chow tests of equal coefficients in adjacent
periods (original Table 4.11). Table~\ref{tbl-chow} shows them next to
three recalculations. In every model and pair, the published \emph{F}
statistic exceeds the largest recalculated one by a factor of at least
\(10^{3.0}\) (W3-13). The published degrees of freedom match those of
the same regressions estimated in levels, rather than in differences, in
11 of 12 comparisons. Our interpretation suggests that the published
statistics were computed on regressions in levels, or with the degrees
of freedom of such regressions, rather than on the estimating equations
in differences. The degrees of freedom do not explain the size of the
published statistics, which no variant comes close to. We report the
published Chow statistics as not reproduced, and we assess the article's
conclusion of structural change (conclusion 2, p.~342) with the robust
test of Section~\ref{sec-pooled}.

\section{Corrections}\label{sec-corrections}

We take the twelve problems listed in the replication plan one at a
time, and add one correction outside it, a constant in stage 2 of Model
I (Section~\ref{sec-stage2-const}; W4-20). For each, we state what the
original did, why that is a problem, what we do instead, and what
changes. The data and model changes are cumulative, and we assess their
combined effect in Section~\ref{sec-effect}.

\subsection{Standard errors labelled as
t-statistics}\label{standard-errors-labelled-as-t-statistics}

The article describes the numbers in parentheses below each coefficient
as t-statistics. The significance marks printed in the same table allow
a direct test. If the parentheses held t-statistics, the marks would
follow from those numbers; if they held standard errors, the marks would
follow from the ratio of coefficient to parenthesis. Read as standard
errors, the parentheses reproduce the printed marks in 42 of 44 cells
(95\%); read as t-statistics, in 22 (50\%). The numbers in parentheses
are standard errors. The cells in which the standard-error reading does
not reproduce the printed mark are, with the \emph{t}-statistic they
imply: Model I, Period 1, DER\_hathat (implied \emph{t} = 1.75); Model
I, Period 4, DFDB (implied \emph{t} = 1.67). In each of them the implied
\emph{p}-value lies between 0.05 and 0.10. Our rule counts such an
estimate as significant (W1-07, W4-16), but the legend of the original
tables defines marks only from the 5\% level, so the original leaves it
unmarked. The mismatch is therefore a matter of the marking threshold,
not evidence against the standard-error reading; the same threshold
explains the one Model I cell that is not reproduced
(Section~\ref{sec-reproduction}) and the unmarked published Period 1
expectation coefficient (W4-28).

\subsection{Units: level coefficients and log
differences}\label{units-level-coefficients-and-log-differences}

All variables of \ref{eq-stage1} to \ref{eq-model2} are first
differences of \emph{levels}, so each coefficient is measured in the
units of the variables in Table~\ref{tbl-variables}, for example
reserves per unit change of the exchange rate. The article reads the
coefficients as percentages, for example ``DFER would increase by
\ldots\% when DFDB increased by 1\%'' (p.~340), and reads the Period 4
NDF coefficient in the same way (p.~340). Because the variables are
first differences of levels, a coefficient is the change in reserves, in
hundred million US dollars, per unit change of the regressor in its own
units. It is a percentage only if both variables are in logarithms. To
give the coefficients a unit-free reading, all models were re-estimated
with log differences of the strictly positive series (FER, the exchange
rate, the NDF, GDP, FDB and FDI). The expectation coefficient then is an
elasticity; net exports, the balance of payments, inflation and the
interest rate stay in level differences (row 6 of Tables
\ref{tbl-steps-1} and \ref{tbl-steps-2}).

\subsection{Sign convention}\label{sign-convention}

Both expectation measures are quoted in RMB per US dollar, so a rise is
an expected \emph{depreciation} of the RMB. A positive coefficient
therefore means that reserves grow faster when a depreciation is
expected, and a negative one that they grow faster when an appreciation
is expected. The published Period 4 NDF coefficient of Model II is
negative and significant (Table~\ref{tbl-repro-b}). The article
describes it in four ways. The abstract speaks of a positive correlation
(p.~327). Section 4.3.1 calls it a ``statistically significant positive
impact'' and, in the next sentence, says that DFER increases ``when DNDF
decrease{[}s{]}'' (p.~340). Section 4.3.2 calls it a ``statistically
significant negative impact'' (p.~341). The first conclusion reads it as
``one unit appreciation in RMB caused \ldots{} unit increases in foreign
exchange reserve'' (p.~342). Under the convention above, the table,
Section 4.3.2 and the first conclusion agree: reserves grew faster when
the forward market priced a stronger renminbi. The abstract and the word
``positive'' on p.~340 contradict the table.

\subsection{Generated regressors}\label{generated-regressors}

In Model I, the stage-3 regressor is estimated in stages 1 and 2. The
conventional stage-3 standard errors treat it as data and so ignore the
sampling variation of the earlier stages (Pagan 1984; Murphy and Topel
1985). A bootstrap of the whole three-stage chain includes that
variation. Stages 1 to 3 are re-estimated in each of 999 replications,
with seed 20261001 (W4-01). The primary scheme is a wild bootstrap
(Mammen 1993; Davidson and Flachaire 2008): the residuals of stage 1 and
the innovations of stage 3 are multiplied by the same Rademacher draw in
each month, which preserves their correlation, and the AR(1) errors of
Periods 1 and 3 are rebuilt from the sign-flipped innovations. A
moving-block bootstrap within periods (Künsch 1989) is reported as a
check. Every coefficient was estimated in at least 968 of 999
replications.

For the headline specification on deflated data, the ratio of the
bootstrap to the conventional standard error of the expectation
coefficient is, by period: Period 1 0.96, Period 2 4.48, Period 3 0.80,
Period 4 0.82 (wild) and Period 1 3.03, Period 2 14.41, Period 3 1.16,
Period 4 49.25 (block). Figure~\ref{fig-ci} compares the intervals, and
Figure~\ref{fig-draws} shows the bootstrap distributions. For the
reproduction (row 2 of Table~\ref{tbl-steps-1}), the bootstrap standard
error exceeds the conventional one in 1 of 4 periods with the wild
bootstrap and in 4 of 4 with the block bootstrap. The direction of the
difference therefore depends on the period and the scheme. No
significance mark changes: the class changes in no period (wild) and no
period (block). The corrected data, where the bootstrap matters, are
discussed in Section~\ref{sec-stage2-const}.

\subsection{Stage-2 constant}\label{sec-stage2-const}

This correction is not in the original replication plan and was added
after the first corrected estimates had been seen (W4-20), for the
reasons below, and is reported as a separate step, so that its effect
can be seen. Stage 2 (\ref{eq-stage2}, \ref{eq-stage2b}) has no
constant. A regression without a constant forces its fitted values
through the origin. If the exchange-rate change has a drift, as in the
managed-appreciation periods, the slope must then fit the drift as well
as the month-to-month persistence, and it is biased towards the value
that reproduces the mean (omitted intercept bias). We add a constant to
stage 2 on the corrected data (row 4c of Table~\ref{tbl-steps-1}). For
Model I, the bootstrap, log-difference and pooled rows on the corrected
data build on it. Model II has no stage 2.

With one stage-2 lag, as in the headline specification, the expectation
regressor is \(\hat c + \hat a\,\Delta ER_{t-1}\) with a constant and
\(\hat a_0\,\Delta ER_{t-1}\) without one. Stage 3 has its own constant,
so

\[
\tau_0 + \tau_1\,(\hat c + \hat a\,\Delta ER_{t-1}) = (\tau_0 + \tau_1 \hat c) + \beta\,\Delta ER_{t-1}, \qquad \beta = \tau_1 \hat a ,
\]

and stage 3 fits the same model with either regressor. Only the units of
the coefficient change: \(\hat\tau_1=\hat\beta/\hat a\), with a standard
error scaled by the same factor. The stage-2 constant therefore leaves
the \emph{t}-statistic and the \emph{p}-value of the expectation
coefficient unchanged and multiplies the coefficient by
\(\hat a_0/\hat a\). In Table~\ref{tbl-steps-1}, rows 4 and 4c have the
same \emph{p}-value in 3 of 3 periods with an identified coefficient.
The coefficient changes by the factor -7.11 in Period 2, 2.48 in Period
3 and 1.58 in Period 4: from 1,617.78 (SE 5,345.07) to -11,500.37 (SE
37,996.62), from 3,831.09** (SE 1,219.03) to 9,497.90** (SE 3,022.19),
and from 2,528.37 (SE 4,448.86) to 3,997.40 (SE 7,033.72).

The same identity explains the bootstrap. Because
\(\hat\tau_1 = \hat\beta/\hat a\), its sampling distribution has long
tails when \(\hat a\) is close to zero relative to its sampling error:
some replications divide by a number near zero. Recovered from rows 4
and 4c by the identity, the stage-2 slope with a constant is -0.015,
0.331 and 0.364 in Periods 2 to 4. Without the constant it is 0.108,
0.820 and 0.575 (standard errors 0.100, 0.127 and 0.122). The ratio of
the wild-bootstrap to the conventional standard error of the expectation
coefficient (row 5) is 1.74, 78.29 and 76.84 in Periods 2 to 4.

Our interpretation suggests that, once a constant absorbs the drift,
little month-to-month persistence of the exchange-rate change remains
within a regime, so the size of a Model I expectation effect is weakly
identified. In Period 3 the coefficient is insignificant under the wild
bootstrap with the constant and insignificant without it, so its
bootstrap significance does not depend on the constant either.

\subsection{Year-to-date GDP}\label{year-to-date-gdp}

In the appendix, the GDP of every month of a quarter is the cumulative
GDP of the year to date. Its first difference is therefore zero in two
months of each quarter, positive at each quarter start, and strongly
negative every January, when the cumulation restarts. This January fall
is an accounting artefact that enters both models as a regressor. The
correction replaces it by discrete quarterly GDP (the difference of
consecutive year-to-date values), held in each month of the quarter
(W4-05). The code first verifies that the appendix value is constant
within every quarter. The effect is shown in row 3 of
Table~\ref{tbl-steps-1} and Table~\ref{tbl-steps-2}, to be compared with
row 1b, the reproduction on the same (raw) data. Under the headline
specification, GDP enters neither stage 2 nor stage 3 of Model I, so
rows 1b and 3 of Table~\ref{tbl-steps-1} coincide; the correction
matters for Model II, where GDP is a regressor.

\subsection{Deflator}\label{sec-deflator}

The article divides every variable by \(\pi/100\). Here \(\pi\) is the
consumer price index with the same month of the previous year = 100, a
year-on-year growth factor \(P_t/P_{t-12}\) rather than a price level
(National Bureau of Statistics of China 2024). Dividing by it gives
\(X_t P_{t-12}/P_t\): the real value multiplied by last year's price
level, not a real series. The correction divides the value variables
(FER, GDP, net exports, FDI, external debt and the balance of payments)
by a fixed-base consumer price index, rebased to 2000 = 100, from the
IMF. The exchange rate and the NDF are not deflated: they are prices of
one currency in another, and a real exchange rate would require both
countries' price levels (W4-05).\footnote{The US-dollar variables are
  thereby deflated by China's consumer prices, which measures their
  purchasing power over Chinese goods.}

The article divides the exchange rates, the balance of payments, GDP,
reserves, net exports, foreign debt, FDI and the NDF by \(\pi\), but not
the interest rate, which it labels real (p.~334). The deflated data
version of this replication divides every model variable except \(\pi\)
itself, including the interest rate, as the replication plan specifies
(W4-24). The interest rate enters only in stage 1. Under the headline's
stage 2 (the alternative form), stage 1 does not enter the expectation
regressor, so the difference does not affect the headline Model I
estimates or Model II. It does affect the stage-1 regressions, and with
them the Durbin-Watson comparison of Section~\ref{sec-reproduction} and
the BOP selection rule (W2-03).

We check the price data in two ways (Table~\ref{tbl-cpi} and
Figure~\ref{fig-cpi}). First, we compare the original \(\pi\) with the
same-month year-on-year index published by the National Bureau of
Statistics (NBS): 144 of 144 months agree within the printed decimals,
with a largest gap of 0.000 points (W4-14). Every month agrees. The
original \(\pi\) is therefore the NBS published index with the same
month of the previous year = 100, a growth factor, which confirms the
premise of this correction. Second, the year-on-year rates implied by
the IMF index agree with the published ones within the printed decimals
in 31 of 144 months (largest gap 0.592 points).\footnote{We compared the
  FRED series with the IMF's in the same way (largest year-on-year gap
  0.0000). The FRED series carries the same index as the IMF's, rebased
  (their ratio is constant up to rounding; W4-05). It therefore confirms
  the download but is not an independent measure.} Our interpretation
suggests, as a hypothesis, that the gaps arise because the published
year-on-year rates and the IMF index are computed from different rounded
inputs: the NBS publishes rates to one decimal, and an index chained
from rounded rates accumulates rounding error. Gaps concentrated around
the years in which the NBS reweights its basket (National Bureau of
Statistics of China 2024) would instead point to the reweighting. We did
not test either explanation, and neither affects the correction, which
uses the index level.

\subsection{Period 1: exchange-rate variation}\label{sec-period1}

From January 2000 to June 2005 the RMB was pegged to the US dollar, and
the appendix exchange rate is constant (standard deviation 0.000 on raw
data). Its change is zero, so no expectation effect can be estimated
from it: the coefficient is not identified (W3-10). In the article's
deflated data the exchange rate varies (standard deviation 0.138), and
that variation comes from the deflator: the \(R^2\) of the deflated
exchange-rate change on the change of \(\pi\) is 0.999
(Table~\ref{tbl-period1}). A Period 1 ``expectation effect'' estimated
on deflated data is therefore an effect of inflation. With the exchange
rate undeflated (the corrected data), the Period 1 coefficient of Model
I is reported as not identified. Model II is not affected: the NDF is a
market price that varies in Period 1, and its coefficient is estimated
in every row (on the corrected data it is positive and insignificant).

\subsection{Unit-root test label}\label{unit-root-test-label}

The article and the text of the original document call the unit-root
test an ADF test; the original tables report DF-GLS tests.

\subsection{Chow tests, correctly
specified}\label{chow-tests-correctly-specified}

The classic Chow test (Chow 1960) assumes equal error variances in the
two regimes and no autocorrelation. Both assumptions are doubtful with
monthly data across exchange-rate regimes. The corrected test estimates
one regression over 2000--2011 in which every coefficient is
regime-specific (Section~\ref{sec-pooled}), and tests the equality of
all coefficients of two adjacent regimes with a Wald test based on
Newey--West standard errors (last column of Table~\ref{tbl-chow};
Table~\ref{tbl-pooled}). For the corrected data, Model I, the tests
give: regimes 1--2, \emph{F}(4, 117) = 2.95, \emph{p} = 0.023; regimes
2--3, \emph{F}(5, 117) = 3.78, \emph{p} = 0.003; regimes 3--4,
\emph{F}(5, 117) = 0.43, \emph{p} = 0.828. For Model II, in the same
order: \emph{F}(6, 116) = 5.70, \emph{p} \textless{} 0.001; \emph{F}(6,
116) = 14.12, \emph{p} \textless{} 0.001; \emph{F}(6, 116) = 4.12,
\emph{p} \textless{} 0.001. At the 5\% level, the robust test rejects
the equality of all Model I coefficients between Regimes 1 and 2,
rejects it between Regimes 2 and 3, and does not reject it between
Regimes 3 and 4. For Model II, in the same order, the test rejects,
rejects, and rejects equality. Our interpretation suggests that the
regime split is supported where the test rejects. Two reservations
apply. Each test uses a Newey-West covariance that is estimated from few
months in Regime 2 (Section~\ref{sec-pooled}). And a rejection says that
some coefficient changes, not that the expectation coefficient does
(that test is reported in Section~\ref{sec-pooled}).

\subsection{Period 2: pooled model with regime
interactions}\label{sec-pooled}

Period 2 has about a year of monthly observations and five or six
regressors, leaving very few residual degrees of freedom. Following the
replication plan, the four periods are pooled in one regression with
regime-specific constants. In the \emph{full} variant every coefficient
is regime-specific, which gives the same point estimates as separate OLS
regressions for each period, with one covariance matrix for the whole
sample (W4-02). In the original document, the regressions of Periods 1
and 3 have AR(1) errors. The pooled model is estimated by OLS, so its
Period 1 and Period 3 coefficients differ from those of the separate
regressions. A difference between row 7 and the earlier rows in those
periods reflects the error model as well as the standard errors. In the
\emph{expectation-only} variant only the constant and the expectation
term are regime-specific and the controls are common (W4-02). Standard
errors are Newey--West, with a lag of 4 (Newey and West 1987, 1994)
(W4-03).

The Newey-West covariance is built from the products of residuals and
regressors within each regime, so in the short regimes it rests on a
dozen or so monthly products. Its two versions disagree there. For
example, for the NDF coefficient of Model II in Regime 4 on the deflated
data, the automatic-bandwidth standard error (Newey and West 1994) is
0.22 times the rule-lag one. By the condition stated in advance (W4-03),
the rule lag stays primary, and we treat the per-regime significance of
the pooled model as unreliable in the short regimes (W4-32). For the
Model I expectation coefficient in Regime 2 on the deflated data, the
Newey-West standard error is 0.09 times the OLS one, and the coefficient
is positive and significant at row 7. We do not read that significance
as evidence of an effect.

The expectation-only variant is valid only if the controls have the same
effect in every regime. The Wald test of that restriction in the full
model gives \emph{F}(9, 117) = 3.77, \emph{p} \textless{} 0.001 for
Model I and \emph{F}(12, 116) = 2.54, \emph{p} = 0.005 for Model II
(corrected data). The test that the expectation coefficient is the same
in all regimes gives \emph{F}(2, 117) = 0.88, \emph{p} = 0.418 (Model I)
and \emph{F}(3, 116) = 2.04, \emph{p} = 0.113 (Model II). At the 5\%
level, the test rejects equal controls for Model I and rejects them for
Model II. Where it rejects, the expectation-only variant imposes a false
restriction, and the fully interacted variant is the appropriate one.
The test of an equal expectation coefficient in all regimes does not
reject equality for Model I and does not reject it for Model II. In the
fully interacted model on the corrected data, the Regime 2 expectation
coefficient is negative and insignificant in Model I and positive and
insignificant in Model II. Our interpretation suggests that pooling does
not reveal a Period 2 expectation effect that the separate regressions
miss for lack of degrees of freedom.

\textsuperscript{a} Pooled OLS over 2000-2011, data version `corrected',
level form, with regime-specific constants. full: every coefficient
regime-specific (point estimates equal the separate per-period OLS fits;
controls not shown); exp\_only: only the constant and the expectation
term regime-specific, controls common.\\
\textsuperscript{b} Newey-West standard errors in parentheses: Bartlett
kernel, lag floor(4(n/100)\^{}(2/9)), no prewhitening, small-sample
factor n/(n-k); p-values from t(n-k). Results with the automatic
bandwidth are in corr\_pooled\_coefs.csv and corr\_pooled\_tests.csv.\\
\textsuperscript{c} Tests: Wald tests with the Newey-West covariance,
reported as F = W/q with (q, n-k) degrees of freedom. A term not
identified in a regime is left out of the restrictions.\\
\textsuperscript{d} Model I uses the stage-3 expected change of the
exchange rate from the headline specification, with a constant in stage
2 (decision W4-20); its pooled standard errors do not account for the
generated regressor (see Table 3 for the bootstrap).\\
\textsuperscript{e} Significance (legend of original Tables 4.9-4.10): †
p \textless{} 0.1, * p \textless{} 0.05, ** p \textless{} 0.01, *** p
\textless{} 0.001 (two-sided; normal for the ARIMA periods, t for
OLS).\\
\textsuperscript{f} Periods: 1 = 2000-01 to 2005-06; 2 = 2005-07 to
2006-05; 3 = 2006-06 to 2008-05; 4 = 2008-06 to 2011-12 (Replication
Plan s.3.3).

\subsection{Interest-rate label}\label{interest-rate-label}

The article calls the interest rate ``real''. The appendix series is the
nominal one-year deposit rate; Table~\ref{tbl-variables} uses the
correct label. The label does not affect any estimate.

\subsection{NDF source}\label{ndf-source}

The article does not state the source of its NDF series. We use the
12-month USD/CNY NDF from Wind, identified by its Wind code in
Table~\ref{tbl-sources} (W2-06).\footnote{Wind lists the series as
  compiled from news reports; its 1-, 3- and 6-month tenors start only
  at the end of 2008, so the 12-month tenor is the only one covering the
  sample.}

\subsection{Corrected estimates}\label{corrected-estimates}

Table~\ref{tbl-corrected} reports both models on the corrected data
(discrete quarterly GDP, fixed-base CPI, exchange rates undeflated) with
the original study's estimators. For Model I it also shows the
wild-bootstrap standard error of the expectation term.

\renewcommand*{\arraystretch}{1}

\textsuperscript{a} Data version `corrected': discrete quarterly GDP;
FER, GDP, NX, FDI, FDB and BOP divided by the IMF consumer price index
(2000 = 100); exchange rates, inflation and the interest rate not
deflated.\\
\textsuperscript{b} Model I: alternative stage-2 regressor, 1 lag(s)
(the headline specification), with a constant in stage 2 (step 4c, a
correction outside Plan s.5; decision W4-20); same estimators as the
original (ARIMA(1,0,0) by ML in Periods 1 and 3, OLS in Periods 2 and
4).\\
\textsuperscript{c} Second line: conventional standard error (OPG in the
ARIMA periods). Third line, expectation term of Model I only:
wild-bootstrap standard error of the whole three-stage chain (999
replications), marks from the centred bootstrap p-value.\\
\textsuperscript{d} n.i. = not identified (no variation of the exchange
rate under the Period 1 peg when it is not deflated).\\
\textsuperscript{e} Significance (legend of original Tables 4.9-4.10): †
p \textless{} 0.1, * p \textless{} 0.05, ** p \textless{} 0.01, *** p
\textless{} 0.001 (two-sided; normal for the ARIMA periods, t for
OLS).\\
\textsuperscript{f} Periods: 1 = 2000-01 to 2005-06; 2 = 2005-07 to
2006-05; 3 = 2006-06 to 2008-05; 4 = 2008-06 to 2011-12 (Replication
Plan s.3.3).

\section{Effect on the conclusions}\label{sec-effect}

Tables \ref{tbl-steps-1} (Model I) and \ref{tbl-steps-2} (Model II)
follow the expectation coefficient through the corrections, one step at
a time. Row 0 is the published value, row 1 our reproduction, and row 1b
the same on raw data. Row 1c is a diagnostic, not a correction: the
reproduction with a constant in stage 2 (W4-19); the headline remains
row 1. Rows 3 to 6 each add one correction to the row before, with the
comparisons 1 → 1b (the article's deflation), 1b → 3 (GDP), 3 → 4
(fixed-base CPI), 4 → 4c (a constant in stage 2, a correction outside
the replication plan; W4-20) and 4c → 6 (log differences). Rows 2 and 5
replace the conventional standard errors by bootstrap ones, and row 7 is
the pooled model; for Model I on the corrected data, rows 5 to 7 include
the stage-2 constant. Model II has no stage 2, so rows 1c and 4c do not
apply to it, and its row 6 follows row 4.

We read the tables with the rule of the reproduction (W1-07). A step
changes the result if the coefficient gains or loses significance, or
changes sign while either estimate is significant. Along the main chain
(row 0, 1, 1b, 3, 4, 4c and 6; Model II without 4c), and from row 1 or
row 4c (Model II: row 4) to the wild bootstrap (row 2 and 5) and the
fully interacted pooled model (row 7), the changes are (W4-30):

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  Model I, Period 1: no change; Period 2: significance gained at 1 → 7
  (full deflated); Period 3: significance gained at 3 → 4; significance
  lost at 4c → 5 (wild); significance lost at 4c → 7 (full corrected);
  Period 4: no change.
\item
  Model II, Period 1: no change; Period 2: no change; Period 3: no
  change; Period 4: significance lost at 1 → 7 (full deflated).
\end{itemize}

\textsuperscript{a} Coefficient on the stage-3 expected change of the
exchange rate (Model I) in each period (step 7: in each regime of the
pooled model), standard error below in parentheses.\\
\textsuperscript{b} Step 0: original, with the significance marks as
printed and the numbers in parentheses read as standard errors (decision
W4-28). Step 1: our reproduction (headline data version). Step 1b: the
same on raw data. Steps 3-6 add one correction at a time (README\_week4
s.2.1): 1b to 3 isolates the GDP correction, 3 to 4 the fixed-base
CPI.\\
\textsuperscript{c} Step 1c is a diagnostic, not a correction (decision
W4-19): the reproduction of step 1 re-estimated with a constant in stage
2, to test whether the original's stage 2 had one; the reproduction
itself (step 1) is unchanged. Step 4c is a correction outside Plan s.5
(decision W4-20): step 4 with a constant in stage 2; for Model I, steps
5, 6 and the pooled model on the corrected data build on 4c.\\
\textsuperscript{d} Steps 2 and 5: bootstrap standard error of the whole
three-stage chain (999 replications; wild: Rademacher; block: moving
blocks within periods); marks from the centred bootstrap p-value.\\
\textsuperscript{e} Step 6: coefficients are elasticities (log
differences of FER and of the exchange-rate terms). Step 7: pooled OLS
2000-2011 with regime-specific constants and expectation terms; full =
all coefficients regime-specific; exp\_only = controls common;
Newey-West standard errors.\\
\textsuperscript{f} n.i. = not identified.\\
\textsuperscript{g} Significance (legend of original Tables 4.9-4.10): †
p \textless{} 0.1, * p \textless{} 0.05, ** p \textless{} 0.01, *** p
\textless{} 0.001 (two-sided; normal for the ARIMA periods, t for
OLS).\\
\textsuperscript{h} Periods: 1 = 2000-01 to 2005-06; 2 = 2005-07 to
2006-05; 3 = 2006-06 to 2008-05; 4 = 2008-06 to 2011-12 (Replication
Plan s.3.3).

\textsuperscript{a} Coefficient on DNDF (Model II) in each period (step
7: in each regime of the pooled model), standard error below in
parentheses.\\
\textsuperscript{b} Step 0: original, with the significance marks as
printed and the numbers in parentheses read as standard errors (decision
W4-28). Step 1: our reproduction (headline data version). Step 1b: the
same on raw data. Steps 3-6 add one correction at a time (README\_week4
s.2.1): 1b to 3 isolates the GDP correction, 3 to 4 the fixed-base
CPI.\\
\textsuperscript{c} Steps 1c and 4c (a stage-2 constant, decisions W4-19
and W4-20) concern stage 2 of Model I; Model II has no stage 2, so they
do not apply (---).\\
\textsuperscript{d} No bootstrap for Model II: its expectation regressor
is observed, not generated.\\
\textsuperscript{e} Step 6: coefficients are elasticities (log
differences of FER and of the exchange-rate terms). Step 7: pooled OLS
2000-2011 with regime-specific constants and expectation terms; full =
all coefficients regime-specific; exp\_only = controls common;
Newey-West standard errors.\\
\textsuperscript{f} n.i. = not identified.\\
\textsuperscript{g} Significance (legend of original Tables 4.9-4.10): †
p \textless{} 0.1, * p \textless{} 0.05, ** p \textless{} 0.01, *** p
\textless{} 0.001 (two-sided; normal for the ARIMA periods, t for
OLS).\\
\textsuperscript{h} Periods: 1 = 2000-01 to 2005-06; 2 = 2005-07 to
2006-05; 3 = 2006-06 to 2008-05; 4 = 2008-06 to 2011-12 (Replication
Plan s.3.3).

For reference, the Model II coefficient of Period 4 is -1,294.45* (SE
531.20) as published, -1,288.88* (SE 531.10) in our reproduction,
-1,545.27** (SE 491.41) on the corrected data, and -0.471** (SE 0.165)
as an elasticity. For Model I, Period 3, it is -200.07 (SE 476.61) as
published, 1,206.45 (SE 895.23) reproduced, and 9,497.90** (SE 3,022.19)
corrected (with the stage-2 constant), with a wild-bootstrap standard
error of 236,604.08 (\emph{p} = 0.593).

\textbf{Model II, Period 4.} The one expectation coefficient that the
article reports as significant is significant at the steps 0 (-), 1 (-),
1b (-), 3 (-), 4 (-), 6 (-), 7 (full corrected) (-), 7 (exp\_only
corrected) (-) (step, sign). It keeps its significance and its sign
through every per-period correction: the article's deflation, the GDP
construction, the deflator and the log form. It loses significance only
in the pooled model on the deflated data, where the per-regime standard
errors are unreliable (W4-32). Its sign is negative, opposite to the
abstract's ``positive'' (Section~\ref{sec-corrections}). Our
interpretation suggests that this finding is robust to the corrections.
It is an association between reserve growth and a market price observed
at the time, not necessarily a causal effect (W4-21).

\textbf{Model I.} None of the published Model I expectation coefficients
is significant (row 0). The Period 3 coefficient is significant at the
steps 4 (+), 4c (+), 6 (+), that is, on the corrected data with
conventional standard errors. It fails three checks. It is insignificant
under the wild bootstrap of the whole three-stage chain (row 5), and
insignificant without the stage-2 constant
(Section~\ref{sec-stage2-const}). It is negative and insignificant in
the pooled model, which estimates Period 3 by OLS instead of with AR(1)
errors. And it depends on the differencing convention (below). The
Period 2 coefficient is significant at 7 (full deflated) (+), the pooled
model on the deflated data, where the Newey-West standard error is
unreliable (W4-32). Period 1 cannot be assessed: on the raw and
corrected data, the exchange rate is constant, and on the deflated data,
its variation comes from the deflator (Section~\ref{sec-period1}). Our
interpretation suggests that Model I gives no robust evidence of an
expectation effect in any period, and that the size of such an effect
would in any case be weakly identified (Section~\ref{sec-stage2-const}).

\textbf{Sensitivity to the differencing convention.} Under the
convention fixed before estimation (first differences on the whole
series; Section~\ref{sec-reproduction}), 8 cells of the step tables
differ in significance, or in a significant sign, from the results above
(W4-31): Model I, Period 2, step 7 (full corrected); Model I, Period 2,
step 7 (full deflated); Model I, Period 3, step 1b; Model I, Period 3,
step 3; Model I, Period 3, step 4; Model I, Period 3, step 4c; Model I,
Period 3, step 6; Model I, Period 3, step 7 (full corrected). No Model
II cell differs. The headline Model I specification also differs between
the conventions (W3-07, W4-27). Our interpretation suggests that the
Model I results are fragile, while the Model II result is robust and
does not depend on how the period samples are built.

\textbf{The article's statements.} (1) The significant recent effect of
the first conclusion survives for the NDF in Period 4. Its sign agrees
with the published table (p.~339), Section 4.3.2 (p.~341) and the first
conclusion (p.~342), but not with the abstract's ``positive''
correlation (p.~327) or the word ``positive'' in Section 4.3.1 (p.~340);
we read those two descriptions as errors (Section~\ref{sec-corrections},
sign convention). Its counterpart, no significant effect before June
2006, holds for every per-period estimate of Periods 1 and 2, but Model
I cannot be assessed in Period 1. (2) The regime explanation of the
signs (p.~341) rests on coefficients that are insignificant in the
original and in our estimates, and their signs carry no conclusion
(W1-07). (3) The structural change of the second conclusion cannot rest
on the published Chow statistics, which are not reproduced. The robust
test of Section~\ref{sec-corrections} bears on it. (4) The policy
recommendation of the third conclusion presupposes a causal effect that
single-equation estimates of this kind cannot establish (W4-21). Model
I's expectation is an in-sample fitted value that uses information from
after the month it describes (W4-17), which we identify as a limitation
of Model I.

\section{Reproducibility and data availability}\label{sec-availability}

Every result in this note is produced by the code in the replication
package, https://github.com/du-jc/fer-replication, at commit 8bf3498,
with R version 4.5.3 (2026-03-11 ucrt) and the package versions recorded
in \texttt{renv.lock}; the tables and figures were generated on
2026-10-08. The code starts from the raw files and runs in a fixed
order; each script writes a run log. The bootstrap uses a fixed seed, so
reruns give identical draws.\footnote{Python collects and registers the
  data; R estimates. Each script writes a run log to
  \texttt{outputs/logs/}.} A complete run from a fresh clone of the
repository, with only the original document and the Wind export added,
reproduced every result table and figure: identical, or identical up to
the order of rows and the record of the run's date, commit and time
stamps (W4-38).

Public data (SAFE, IMF, FRED, NBS) are included in the package with
their download dates and checksums (Table~\ref{tbl-sources}). Two inputs
are not included. The original document is unpublished work of the
original authors; its reproduction targets (the numbers of its tables)
are included in the package as \texttt{original\_targets.csv} (W4-08).
The Wind NDF series is proprietary and is required to build the analysis
panel: without it, the first estimation script
(\texttt{01\_import\_verify.R}) stops, and no estimate can be reproduced
(W4-23). A reader with Wind access can export the series using the code
in Table~\ref{tbl-sources}, register it with \texttt{register\_wind.py},
and run the package unchanged.

\section{Conclusion}\label{sec-conclusion}

We reproduced the published estimates of Du, Ge and Qiao (2014) from the
data of the original document. Of the 20 Model I estimates, 18 are
reproduced under our rule, and of the Model II estimates 23 of 24. Many
of these agree because both the published and our estimates are
insignificant (Section~\ref{sec-reproduction}). The controls and the
autoregressive error terms reproduce closely. Model I's expectation term
reproduces only in that weak sense, because it is a generated regressor
built from choices the original does not report. The published constants
are consistent with variables divided by \(\pi\) rather than
\(\pi/100\), a diagnosis found after the results were seen (W4-26). The
Durbin-Watson, Breusch-Godfrey and Chow statistics are not reproduced,
while 124 of 168 Durbin-Watson and Breusch-Godfrey test decisions are.

We found and corrected errors of three kinds. The first are errors of
reporting: standard errors labelled as \emph{t}-statistics, level
coefficients read as percentages, a sign described inconsistently, and
an ADF label on DF-GLS tests. The second are errors of data
construction: a year-on-year inflation factor used as a deflator,
year-to-date GDP whose differences fall every January, and Period 1
``variation'' in the exchange rate that comes from the deflator. The
third are errors of inference: standard errors that ignore the
estimation of the generated regressor, Chow statistics that cannot be
reproduced, and a period too short to estimate. We also added a constant
to stage 2 (Section~\ref{sec-stage2-const}).

The article's central empirical finding, a significant relation between
the NDF and reserve growth in 2008-2011, survives every per-period
correction. Its sign is negative: reserves grew faster when the forward
market priced appreciation, contrary to the abstract's ``positive''
relation. The model-based expectation of Model I shows no robust effect
in any period, and the regime-based interpretation of the signs rests on
insignificant coefficients (Section~\ref{sec-effect}).

Several questions remain open. Model I's expectation is an in-sample
fitted value: it uses information from after the month it describes
(W4-17). An expectation formed in real time, with the data available at
each date, is the natural test, and it requires longer samples than the
original's periods. The article's conceptual model has reserves
affecting the exchange rate, a loop that single-equation estimates do
not identify (W4-21). Both questions are left for future work with data
for 2000-2025. Within this replication, the original's construction of
its Model I samples (W4-18), the size of its Chow statistics (W3-13) and
the regressions behind its Durbin-Watson statistics remain unexplained.
